UAL — Evidence appendix
All harvested proof points · company first, then peers

Evidence

Claims by section
business-model
  • United sells three things: seat-miles on its own and contracted regional aircraft, belly cargo space on the same flights, and MileagePlus miles to JPMorgan Chase and about 110 other partners.
  • Fees were $4,800m in FY2025, 9.0% of passenger revenue and $26.51 a passenger against $14.77 in 2019, so about a quarter of the per-passenger revenue gain since 2019 came from fees, which the driver tree below cannot separate from premium mix and cost pass-through (10-K FY2025 and FY2019, Note 2).
  • An award seat is booked at 62% of what a paid seat over the same distance produces, and because the redemption line also covers upgrades and partner-airline redemptions while the mile count covers free flights only, 12.20 cents is a maximum.
  • Passenger revenue has been below total operating expense in every year after 2019.
  • On this allocation every dollar of FY2025 operating income came from those two lines while the flying ran $918m short of its fully-allocated cost, against a $667m surplus in 2019.
  • Over the three years to 2025, revenue rose $14,115m and non-fuel operating expense rose $13,455m, so 4.7 cents of each incremental dollar reached operating income before any fuel effect; in 2024-2025 the same calculation is negative 37.0%.
  • PRASM was 16.15 cents in 2022 and 16.18 cents in 2025, and over 2023-2025 it declined from 16.84c to 16.18c, so volume contributed 150% of the passenger revenue change and price -50% (10-K FY2022 and FY2025 operating statistics).
  • Loyalty revenue was therefore $6,614m in FY2025, 11.2% of total revenue, against $4,213m and 9.7% in 2019, a 7.8% compound rate `[estimate: two disclosed GAAP lines summed, validated against three management statements]`.
  • Third-party mile sales therefore generated roughly $5.8-6.4bn of cash in FY2025, about 10% of revenue `[estimate: the 2019 disclosed cash split scaled by recognised partner revenue, cross-checked against the frequent-flyer deferral]`.
  • Solving the disclosed cabin growth rates against that base gives premium revenue of roughly $18-21bn, 34-39% of passenger revenue `[estimate: management's cabin growth rates solved against reported cabin-fare revenue]`.
  • Advance ticket sales and frequent-flyer deferred revenue were $15,908m at YE2025, 26.9% of revenue, and $18,723m at 30 June 2026; receivables were $2,391m, about 15 days of revenue, because customers pay by card before they fly.
capitalization
  • Reporting net leverage below 2.0x at 31 December 2026 with adjusted net debt held at the June level needs full-year adjusted EBITDAR above $9,053m. H1-2026 produced $3,647m, so H2 must deliver $5,406m against $4,726m in H2-2025, an increase of 14.4%
competitive-advantage
  • United earns returns above its cost of capital in the up-cycle and roughly at its cost of capital across the cycle.
  • Across 2016-2025 it averaged 7.9%, an average that includes $7.5bn of federal Payroll Support Program grants booked as credits to operating expense in 2020-2021.
  • Hub scale at Newark, Houston, Denver and San Francisco with immunised joint businesses on the Atlantic and Pacific gives United the largest international network of any US carrier at a unit cost below both network peers: CASM-ex of 12.64 cents in 2025 against 13.86 at Delta and 14.12 at American, each on its own definition.
  • MileagePlus is a growing annuity: partner revenue recognised in other operating revenue rose from $1.7bn in 2016 to $3.2bn in 2025 and deferred revenue from $4,883m to $7,777m.
  • Pricing power in the base cabin is absent: passenger unit revenue rose 16.4% from 2019 to 2025 while non-fuel unit cost rose 23.8%
  • United's parity with Delta on margin is a cost outcome: Delta earns 19% more revenue per seat-mile and spends 17% more per seat-mile.
  • United's 2025 operating margin and return were second among the six US carriers, about a point behind Delta and 5-12 points ahead of the other four.
  • That premium widened because those four collapsed — Southwest from 13.2% to 1.5%, Alaska from 12.1% to 2.1%, JetBlue from 9.9% to -4.1% — more than because United improved, since United's own margin went from 9.9% to 8.0%.
  • Delta is the same model with a larger loyalty annuity and majority-share hubs in Atlanta, Detroit and Minneapolis; Delta's 2016-2019 return averaged 21.9% against United's 13.9% and the two converged to 12.8% and 12.6% in 2025, so United is the weaker twin on revenue quality and the equal on cost.
competitive-landscape
  • United is the scale leader in international flying and in cargo, the smallest of the big three in loyalty, and fourth of four in the US domestic market where two-thirds of industry capacity is flown.
  • United's domestic passenger revenue of about $31,500m (58.9% of passenger revenue per the quarterly releases) is 20-22% of that pool, and its disclosed domestic revenue of $35,017m, which is total operating revenue by region and therefore includes cargo, partner revenue and Canada-origin traffic, is 23-24%.
  • On BTS domestic RPMs the shares are Delta 17.9%, American 17.6%, Southwest 17.0%, United 17.0%, Alaska 6.8%, JetBlue 4.6%, Frontier 3.8%, Spirit 2.7%, SkyWest 2.6% and Envoy 1.2%, an HHI near 1,306, which is unconcentrated by the DOJ and FTC thresholds.
  • Price is set by whoever will fly an unprofitable seat, which is why cost harmonisation matters more than capacity discipline in 2026.
  • American, Delta and United accounted for 93% of US industry operating profit and 95% of pre-tax profit in the June 2025 quarter, and Delta and United alone were expected to produce 74% and 80% of industry operating and pre-tax profit for 2025 [web, ALPA October 2025].
  • United grew faster than Delta in all three international regions and slower at home. Its Pacific network is larger than Delta's and American's combined and is compounding at nearly three times Delta's rate.
  • United's cargo revenue was 1.57x Delta's in 2019 and 1.98x in 2025, on ton-miles up only 8.9% over six years, so about four-fifths of the growth is yield.
  • United's deferred balance is 16% below Delta's and 26% below American's and its recognised loyalty revenue 13% below both, while growing 13.9% a year over three years against 11.4% and 9.2%.
  • OAG's September 2026 US schedule shows Alaska seats up 30%, Breeze up 46%, Frontier up 23% and JetBlue up 14% year over year, against United up 5% and Delta up 1%
  • OAG's summer 2026 schedule count, published in May 2026, puts United at 9.72m transatlantic seats, Delta at 9.68m, American at 7.19m, British Airways at 6.24m, Lufthansa at 5.10m and Air France at 4.51m; across the twenty carriers in that table United holds about 14%
  • United held capacity growth at 3.5% while American grew 5.4% and lost 1.5 points of load factor for 10.0% of passenger unit-revenue growth against United's 12.5%.
critical-points
  • Reported Q2-2026 operating income declined 17.3% year over year; excluding special items it declined 46.3%, from $1,772m to $951m. On a trailing-twelve-month basis reported operating income is 3.4% above FY2025 and the ex-special figure is 14.3% below.
  • A 10% pre-tax margin on FY2025 revenue is $5,907m against the $4,306m reported, a $1,601m gap equal to 0.48 cents of unit revenue. That requires a TRASM-to-CASM spread of about 1.91 cents; the widest spread in the 2019-2025 record is 1.64 cents.
  • Consensus puts FY2027 operating margin at 9.8% against FY2025's 8.0%, a 1.8-point gain two years after a fuel spike; across the eleven observations of the two prior spikes the median outcome was 0.9 points below the pre-spike margin and seven of eleven ended lower. Measured from the H1-2026 run-rate of 6.5% the required move is 3.3 points, beyond every observation in that set. No US carrier has reached a 10% operating margin since 2021, and United has cleared a 10% pre-tax margin twice in eighteen years, in 2015 and 2016, at fuel under $2.00 a gallon.
expectations
  • Consensus FY2027 revenue growth of 4.8% and an operating margin of 9.8% together imply fuel near $3.50 a gallon. Under management's own retention framing, if fuel goes to $3.50 or below then part of the 2026 fare increase is given back and revenue undershoots $71,183m; if fuel stays above it the margin does. The two consensus lines are mutually consistent only inside a narrow band.
financial-history
  • Trailing twelve months to 30 June 2026 against FY2025: revenue $62,902m, +6.5% and accelerating; operating income $4,873m as reported, +3.4%, against $4,259m excluding special items, down 14.3%; the fuel price $2.98 against $2.44, +22%.
  • The spread narrowed 0.09 cents on a much larger base, and the composition inverted: fuel unit cost rose only 0.31 cents while non-fuel unit cost rose 2.48 cents and unit revenue 2.70 cents. The post-2019 compression is non-fuel cost, of which labour is the largest piece: salaries per seat-mile went from 4.24 to 5.34 cents while headcount grew 18.0% to 113,200 and pay per employee 23.8%.
forward-model
  • Operating margin recovers from 5.9% in 2026 to 8.4% by 2028 and then flattens between 9.3% and 9.7%, so the terminal adjusted pre-tax margin of 9.3% is below management's stated 2027 floor and well below the mid-teens aspiration.
  • Correcting both removes roughly $3.5bn from FY2032 operating income, which would take the terminal operating margin from 9.7% to under 6%.
  • Solving the model for the consensus FY2027 EPS of $15.06 requires structural unit-revenue growth of 5.7% instead of 2.2%; solving it for management's 10% floor requires 8.0% and produces $17.75 of adjusted EPS. United's realised unit-revenue growth was 2.6% a year over 2019-2025 and zero over 2022-2025.
m-and-a
  • It has bought one business of consequence in sixteen years, the all-stock Continental merger closed on 1 October 2010, plus a Phoenix flight school in 2020 at an undisclosed price.
  • Where capital has been lost is the minority-stake and partner-loan book: about $1.1bn of disclosed cash went into Azul, the Avianca ownership chain, ExpressJet's parent and Mesa between 2015 and 2023, with a further $100m subscribed into the reorganised Azul in February 2026, and $925m of that has been recognised as losses.
management-claims
  • Across 103 US airline-years since 2008, 22.3% cleared a 10% pre-tax margin and 9.7% cleared 14%, and all ten mid-teens observations fall in 2015-2018 with jet fuel between $1.25 and $2.03 a gallon. United's own maximum is 11.1%, in 2015.
  • The same target set in June 2021 for 2026 was about 14%; it moved to "update at Investor Day" in January 2024, then to double-digit without a year, then to 2027, and no filing or release retires the original.
  • Renegotiation had not started as of the Q2-2026 call, the contract runs into 2029, and the base against which a doubling would be measured has never been disclosed.
  • On what survives a fuel retracement the CEO has published two numbers: 20% retained (April 2026, conditioned on fuel returning to mid-February levels) and 90% retained (July 2026, conditioned on capacity, with 10% "sort of at risk").
  • consensus implies a 65% incremental operating margin in 2027, the 80th percentile of 41 US airline-years with revenue growth between 2% and 10% from a positive base, and of the eight observations at or above that level, five are carriers in 2015 alone, when Gulf Coast jet spot declined 43.5%
management-incentives
  • Management said in January 2025 it expected net leverage below 2x "during this calendar year", and it was 2.2x at YE2025 and 2.2x at 30 June 2026, because trailing adjusted EBITDAR declined 10.5% on fuel while adjusted net debt declined 1.4%.
secular-trends
  • United's passenger revenue rose 34.9% from 2019 to 2025. Capacity contributed 15.9%, yield 18.9% and load factor -2.1%, and the product of the three reproduces the reported figure. Price did more than volume over six years; over the last three years volume did all of it.
  • United's domestic capacity share was about 18.4% in 2019 and 18.5% in 2025, while its share of US-carrier international capacity rose from about 34% to about 36%
  • United's own realised yield is 5.6% below 2019 in real terms for 2025 and, at Q2-2026's +12.1%, about 3% above 2019 in real terms
technology
  • Two are technology in the strict sense and neither is exclusive to United: Starlink low-earth-orbit Wi-Fi, bought from SpaceX, and the new-generation Boeing and Airbus fleet, bought from the two airframers every peer buys from. The rest are commercial systems or regulatory and geographic positions. United differentiates by how early and how widely it adopts, and by hub geography; each adoption lead has a date on which it closes.
  • Cirium's 2025 review put United fourth in North America on arrivals within 15 minutes at 78.77%, behind Delta at 80.90%, Alaska at 79.20% and Spirit at 78.83%
  • Programs United executes with its own staff have shipped on or near time; programs whose input is a Boeing or Airbus delivery are two to four years late. The 2027 plan — double-digit margin, a gauge step-up, the MAX 10 entering service — depends mostly on the second kind.
value-chain
  • Of each dollar United collected in 2025, unionised labour received 29.9 cents, fuel suppliers 19.3 cents, airports and air traffic control 6.5 cents, engine makers and outside maintenance shops 5.6 cents, aircraft owners 5.4 cents, regional carriers 4.6 cents and distribution intermediaries 3.6 cents. United kept 8.0 cents as operating income and 5.7 cents as net income.
  • The airline keeps 8 cents of the passenger's dollar, and the forwarder keeps 25 cents of the shipper's airfreight dollar and passes 75 to airlines. The engine maker earns 27 cents on its own dollar, most of it from the airline's maintenance line, and the airframer loses 17 cents.
  • Maintenance is United's fastest-growing cost line per seat-mile since 2019 at +58.5%, ahead of other operating at +36.4% and airports at +30.6%; in FY2025 dollars, aircraft rent grew fastest at 30.4% and salaries added the most at $969m. Bargaining power moved to employees, engine makers and airport authorities.
  • In 2026 the constraint on flown capacity is fuel economics and the FAA caps; the constraint on the fleet plan through 2028 is engine supply, which shows in United's numbers as 787 slippage, grounded 777s and a stalled A350 order.
  • If flight-earned miles are 35-50% of the deferral, partner-sold miles are $1,900-2,500m and total partner cash is $5,100-5,700m, 8.6-9.7% of revenue `[estimate: recognised partner revenue plus the partner share of miles deferred]`. Delta's American Express remuneration was $8,200m, 12.9% of revenue, and American's cash from co-brand and other partners $6,200m, 11.3%. At Delta's ratio United would collect about $7,600m, so closing that gap is worth $1,900-2,500m a year.

United sells three things: seat-miles on its own and contracted regional aircraft, belly cargo space on the same flights, and MileagePlus miles to JPMorgan Chase and about 110 other partners.

Domestic passenger revenue was up 20.3% with PRASM up 12.2%. International PRASM was also up 12%. Pacific led the way with PRASM up 14%, Atlantic up 12.1%, and Latin up 10.7%. Cargo revenues were also strong, up 22.6% and loyalty revenue was up 11.3%.
— Andrew P. Nocella, EVP & Chief Commercial Officer, United Airlines Holdings Q2 2026 earnings call, 2026-07-16

filingsupports
United has an agreement with JPMorgan Chase Bank, N.A. ("Chase"), pursuant to which members of United's MileagePlus loyalty program who are residents of the United States can earn miles for making purchases using a MileagePlus credit card issued by Chase (the "Co-Brand Agreement"). The Co-Brand Agreement also provides for joint marketing and other support for the MileagePlus credit card and provides Chase with other benefits such as permission to market to the Company's customer database.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K FY2025, 2026-02-11
filingsupports
The Company provides freight and mail transportation services ("Air Cargo"). The majority of Air Cargo services are provided to commercial businesses, freight forwarders, logistics firms and national postal services. Through our global network, the Company's Air Cargo operations are able to connect the world's major freight gateways. The Company generates Air Cargo revenues in domestic and international markets through the use of cargo capacity on regularly scheduled passenger flights, interline and charter flights, and ground trucking arrangements.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K FY2025, 2026-02-11
filingqualifies
The Company manages its operations as one segment. Managing the Company as one segment allows management the opportunity to maximize the value of its route network and maximize the Company's consolidated financial results. The Company deploys its aircraft across its route network through a single route scheduling system to maximize its value. The Company's chief executive officer is its CODM.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K FY2025, 2026-02-11
filingsupports
In return, the regional carriers operate the capacity of the aircraft included within the scope of such CPA exclusively for United, on schedules determined by the Company. The Company also determines pricing and revenue management, assumes the inventory and distribution risk for the available seats and permits mileage accrual and redemption for regional flights through its MileagePlus loyalty program.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K FY2025, 2026-02-11
filingsupports
We have contracts to sell miles to these partners with the terms extending from approximately one to five years. These partners include domestic and international credit card issuers, retail merchants, hotels, car rental companies and our participating airline partners.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K FY2025, 2026-02-11

presentationsupports
We've been on a long-term journey to build a brand loyal airline that was durable, that had resilience in the revenue, that diversity in the revenue, multiple revenue streams, not just concentrated in one area. And we thought that, that would lead to higher margins in good times and provide a lot more resilience when times were more difficult.

The Company charges fees, separately from ticket sales, for certain ancillary services that are directly related to passenger travel, such as baggage fees, premium seat fees, inflight amenity fees, and other ticket-related fees. These ancillary fees are part of the travel performance obligation and, as such, are recognized as Passenger revenue when the travel occurs. The Company recorded $4.8 billion, $4.5 billion and $4.1 billion of ancillary fees within Passenger revenue in the years ended December 31, 2025, 2024 and 2023, respectively.

deckqualifies

filingqualifies
The Company's estimated selling price of miles is based on an equivalent ticket value, which incorporates the expected redemption of miles, as the best estimate of the selling price for these miles. The equivalent ticket value is based on the prior 12 months' weighted average equivalent ticket value of similar fares as those used to settle award redemptions while taking into consideration such factors as redemption pattern, cabin class, loyalty status and geographic region. The estimated selling price of miles is adjusted by mileage breakage that considers a number of factors, including redemption patterns of various customer groups.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K FY2025, 2026-02-11
filingqualifies
The Company's mileage breakage model is based on the assumption that the likelihood that an account will redeem its miles can be estimated based on a consideration of the account's historical behavior. ... Changes to mileage breakage assumptions, or to program rules and program redemption opportunities, may result in material changes to the deferred revenue balance as well as recognized revenues from the program. We recognize mileage breakage proportionally as the remaining miles are redeemed.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K FY2025, 2026-02-11
filingqualifies
Miles in MileagePlus members' accounts are combined into one homogeneous pool and are thus not separately identifiable, for award redemption purposes, between miles earned in the current period and those in their beginning balance. Of the miles expected to be redeemed, the Company expects the majority of these miles to be redeemed within two years.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K FY2025, 2026-02-11
filingsupports
MileagePlus miles awarded - United has a performance obligation to provide MileagePlus cardholders with miles to be used for air travel and non-travel award redemptions. The Company records Passenger revenue related to the travel awards when the transportation is provided and records Other operating revenue related to the non-travel awards when the goods or services are delivered.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K FY2025, 2026-02-11

Peer: American Airlines (AAL)

filingqualifies
Sales of mileage credits to co-branded credit card and non-airline business partners are comprised of two revenue elements: a transportation component and a marketing component. ... The portion of each mileage credit sold attributable to transportation is initially deferred and then recognized in passenger revenue when mileage credits are redeemed and transportation is provided. The marketing component includes the use of intellectual property, including the American brand and access to loyalty program member lists, which is the predominant element in these agreements, as well as advertising and other travel-related benefits. We recognize the marketing component in other revenue in the period of the mileage credit sale following the sales-based royalty method.
— American Airlines Group 10-K, FY2025, 2026-02-18

It's so intertwined with brand loyalty. So you have a loyalty system. And when you were a smaller airline and you were an airline that maybe wasn't differentiated, it was less compelling. And so I think what you're going to see is the changes we will announce are going to accelerate the card acquisitions to match the growth in our brand-loyal customer base.
— Michael D. Leskinen, EVP & CFO, Barclays 43rd Annual Industrial Select Conference, 2026-02-18

filingsupports
Passenger tickets and related ancillary services sold by the Company for flights are purchased primarily via credit card transactions, with payments collected by the Company in advance of the performance of related services. The Company initially records ticket sales in its Advance ticket sales liability, deferring revenue recognition until the travel occurs. ... Advance ticket sales represent the Company's liability to provide air transportation in the future. All tickets sold at any given point in time have travel dates through the next 12 months.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K, FY2025, 2026-02-12
filingqualifies
The Company has agreements with financial institutions that process customer credit card transactions for the sale of air travel and other services. Under certain of the Company's credit card processing agreements, the financial institutions in certain circumstances have the right to require that the Company maintain a reserve equal to a portion of advance ticket sales that has been processed by that financial institution, but for which the Company has not yet provided the air transportation. Such financial institutions may require additional cash or other collateral reserves to be established or additional withholding of payments related to receivables collected if the Company does not maintain certain minimum levels of unrestricted cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments (collectively, "Unrestricted Liquidity"). The Company's current level of Unrestricted Liquidity is substantially in excess of these minimum levels.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K, FY2025, 2026-02-12

The deficit to Delta closed from 4-6 points of margin and 5-11 points of ROIC in 2016-2019 to about 1 point and zero in 2024-2025. The gap over the domestic-focused four flipped from -1 point of ROIC in 2019 to +11 points in 2024-2025 and widened each year from 2021.
— Competitive advantage workpaper, United Airlines Holdings, 2026-09-11
filingqualifies
The premium widened because the four's margins collapsed (Southwest 13.2% to 1.5%, Alaska 12.1% to 2.1%, JetBlue 9.9% to -4.1% between 2019 and 2025) more than because United's improved (9.9% to 8.0%). A gap that widens while the leader's own margin declines is evidence of relative cost position and revenue mix, not of pricing power.
— Competitive advantage workpaper, United Airlines Holdings, 2026-09-11

earnings callsupports
For some historical perspective, Southwest, historically, was the airline with the highest percentage of brand loyal customers. For most of its history, Southwest was focused on smaller secondary cities where they had by far the best schedule for customers. They also had a huge customer advantage as the only airline that didn't have change fees or back fees. That meant that a high percentage of their customers for brand loyal to Southwest. At its core, that's why Southwest historically was the highest margin airline in good times and while they always outperformed by an even wider margin when times got tough. But as they've moved into big high-cost hubs of other airlines, both of those advantages are gone and now United and one other airline are the brand loyal customer leaders.
— J. Scott Kirby, CEO & Director, United Airlines Holdings, Inc., Q1 2025 Earnings Call, 2025-04-16
earnings callsupports
I think one thing that's becoming even more clear though is also the strength -- the dispersion of results in the industry and the strength of the 2 brand loyal airlines really winning and everyone else losing. And if I dig deeper into it and I look at every airline that's not named United or Delta, I can find at every single one of them, a double-digit percentage of their route network that loses money. And the only way for them to get margins that are anywhere close to their WAC is to stop flying places that lose money. And that is going to ultimately happen.
— J. Scott Kirby, CEO & Director, United Airlines Holdings, Inc., Q2 2025 Earnings Call, 2025-07-17
earnings callsupports
The industry has transformed into a healthier industry where customers are brand loyal and increasingly choose to fly not just based on the schedule, but also based on their preferences for reliability, for clubs, for technology and for loyalty programs. The industry now has 2 brand loyal, structurally profitable and revenue diverse airlines, which has driven much of the rest of the industry to cut money-losing capacity to return to profitability. This is irreversible and will lead to stable double-digit margins for United Airlines.
— Michael D. Leskinen, Executive VP & CFO, United Airlines Holdings, Inc., Q2 2025 Earnings Call, 2025-07-17

The domestic airline industry is highly competitive and dynamic. ... Air carriers' cost structures are not uniform and are influenced by numerous factors. Carriers with lower costs may offer lower fares to passengers, which could have a potential negative impact on the Company's revenues. Domestic pricing decisions are impacted by intense competitive pressure exerted on the Company by other U.S. airlines. Given the highly competitive nature of the airline industry, the Company historically has had limited ability to, and may not be able to in the future, increase its fares and fees sufficiently to offset the full impact of increases in fuel prices, especially if these increases are significant, rapid and sustained. ... In addition, decreases in fuel prices for an extended period of time may result in increased industry capacity, increased competitive actions for market share and lower fares or surcharges.
— United Airlines Holdings 10-K for fiscal year 2025, 2026-02-12

Our revenues are sensitive to the actions of other carriers in many areas, including pricing, scheduling, capacity, fees (including cancellation, change and baggage fees), amenities, loyalty benefits and promotions, which can have a substantial adverse effect on our and industry revenues. These factors may become even more significant when the industry experiences large losses, as airlines under financial stress, or in bankruptcy, may institute pricing or fee structures intended to attract more customers to achieve near-term survival at the expense of long-term viability.

filingsupports
On all of our routes, pricing decisions are affected, in large part, by the need to meet competition from other airlines. Price competition occurs on a market-by-market basis through price discounts, changes in pricing structures, fare matching, targeted promotions and loyalty program initiatives. Airlines typically use discounted fares and other promotions to stimulate traffic during normally weak travel periods, when they begin service to new cities, when they have excess capacity, to generate cash flow, to maximize revenue per available seat mile or to establish, increase or preserve market share. Most airlines will quickly match price reductions in a particular market, and we have often elected to match discounted or promotional fares initiated by other air carriers in certain markets in order to compete in those markets.
— American Airlines Group 10-K for fiscal year 2025, 2026-02-18

filingqualifies
We account for marketing agreements, including those with American Express, by allocating the consideration to the individual products and services delivered. We allocate the value based on the relative selling prices of those products and services, which generally consist of award travel, baggage fee waivers, lounge access, priority boarding and the use of our brand. We determine our best estimate of the selling prices by using a discounted cash flow analysis using multiple inputs and assumptions, including (1) the expected number of miles awarded and number of miles redeemed, (2) ETV for the award travel obligation adjusted for mileage breakage, (3) published rates on our website for baggage fees, lounge access and priority boarding while traveling on Delta, (4) brand value (using estimated royalties generated from the use of our brand) and (5) volume discounts provided to certain partners.
— Delta Air Lines, Inc. Form 10-K for the fiscal year ended December 31, 2025, 2026-02-11

Consensus puts FY2027 operating margin at 9.8% against FY2025's 8.0%, a 1.8-point gain two years after a fuel spike; across the eleven observations of the two prior spikes the median outcome was 0.9 points below the pre-spike margin and seven of eleven ended lower. Measured from the H1-2026 run-rate of 6.5% the required move is 3.3 points, beyond every observation in that set. No US carrier has reached a 10% operating margin since 2021, and United has cleared a 10% pre-tax margin twice in eighteen years, in 2015 and 2016, at fuel under $2.00 a gallon.

Consensus FY2027 revenue growth of 4.8% and an operating margin of 9.8% together imply fuel near $3.50 a gallon. Under management's own retention framing, if fuel goes to $3.50 or below then part of the 2026 fare increase is given back and revenue undershoots $71,183m; if fuel stays above it the margin does. The two consensus lines are mutually consistent only inside a narrow band.

United's realised unit-revenue growth was 2.6% a year over 2019-2025 and zero over 2022-2025.

For United, here's how we're thinking about our goals to get to 100% pass-through and achieve double-digit margins in 2027. One, to recover 100% of fuel costs, yields need to increase by about 15% to 20%, and we are assuming that fuel may remain higher for longer. Two, as yields increase, there will be an elasticity effect on demand that we're estimating will lead to less overall demand. On May 2, 2010, UAL Corporation, Continental and JT Merger Sub Inc., a wholly-owned subsidiary of UAL Corporation, entered into an Agreement and Plan of Merger (the “Merger agreement”). On October 1, 2010, JT Merger Sub Inc. merged with and into Continental, with Continental surviving as a wholly-owned subsidiary of UAL Corporation (the “Merger”). Upon closing of the Merger, UAL Corporation became the parent company of both Continental and United and UAL Corporation’s name was changed to United Continental Holdings, Inc. Pursuant to the terms of the Merger agreement, each outstanding share of Continental common stock was converted into and became exchangeable for 1.05 fully paid and nonassessable shares of UAL common stock with any fractional shares paid in cash.
— UAL 10-K FY2010, Note 2 — Merger, Description of Transaction, 2011-02-22

filingsupports
Auditing management's valuation of the BRW Loan Collateral was highly judgmental due to the significant estimation required in determining the fair value. The fair value estimate was sensitive to significant assumptions such as multiples of enterprise value to EBITDAR, revenue and cost growth rates and the discount rate, each of which is affected by expectations about future market or economic conditions. As a result of the subjectivity of the assumptions, adverse changes to management's estimates could reduce the underlying cash flows used to estimate fair value and trigger impairment of the loan.
— Ernst & Young LLP (independent registered public accounting firm), UAL 10-K FY2019, Report of Independent Registered Public Accounting Firm — Critical Audit Matters, 2020-02-25

Two are technology in the strict sense and neither is exclusive to United: Starlink low-earth-orbit Wi-Fi, bought from SpaceX, and the new-generation Boeing and Airbus fleet, bought from the two airframers every peer buys from. The rest are commercial systems or regulatory and geographic positions. United differentiates by how early and how widely it adopts, and by hub geography; each adoption lead has a date on which it closes.

filingsupports
When performing airfreight services, we typically act as a freight consolidator. When acting as a freight consolidator, we purchase cargo capacity from airlines on a volume basis and resell that space to our customers at lower rates than they could obtain directly from airlines on an individual shipment. We then issue a House Airway Bill (HAWB) to our customers as the contract of carriage and separately, we receive a Master Airway Bill from the airline when the freight is physically tendered.
— EXPD 10-K FY2025, Item 1 Business, Airfreight Services, 2026-02

Expeditors' management believes that owning aircraft would subject us to undue business risks, including large capital outlays, increased fixed operating expenses, exposure to volatile fuel prices and problems of fully utilizing aircraft. We may also be impacted by regulations affecting certain of our major commercial partners, including our co-branded credit card partner, or our loyalty program. For example, there has been bipartisan legislation proposed in Congress called the Credit Card Competition Act designed to increase credit card transaction routing options for merchants which, if enacted, could result in a material reduction of the fees levied on credit card transactions. Additionally, the executive branch recently proposed a temporary 10% cap on credit card interest rates and called on Congress to pass legislation establishing a cap. If either of these proposals were enacted through legislation or regulation, they could fundamentally alter the profitability of our agreement with our co-branded credit card partner and the benefits we provide to our consumers through the co-branded credit cards issued by our partner.
— AAL 10-K FY2025, Item 1A Risk Factors, 2026-02

Peer: Delta Air Lines (DAL)

filingsupports
Miles may be used toward award redemptions such as flights and upgrades on Delta, our regional carriers and other participating airlines as well as donations to specific charities and more. In 2025, 12% of revenue miles flown on Delta were from award travel, as program members redeemed miles in the loyalty program for approximately 35 million award tickets. Our most significant and valuable contract to sell miles relates to our co-brand credit card relationship with American Express. In 2025, remuneration from American Express totaled $8.2 billion, which we expect to grow to $10 billion over the next few years.
— DAL 10-K FY2025, Item 1 Business, SkyMiles Program, 2026-02
filingsupports
During 2025, operating activities generated $8.3 billion, primarily from ticket sales and the sale of SkyMiles to our partners. Remuneration from American Express related to the SkyMiles program were $8.2 billion during 2025, an increase of approximately 11% compared to 2024.
— DAL 10-K FY2025, Item 7 MD&A, cash flows, 2026-02
filingsupports
At December 31, 2025, the aggregate deferred revenue balance associated with the SkyMiles program was $9.3 billion. A hypothetical 10% change in the number of outstanding miles estimated to be redeemed would result in an impact of less than 1% of total operating revenue recognized for the year ended December 31, 2025.
— DAL 10-K FY2025, Critical Accounting Estimates, Loyalty Program, 2026-02

Peer: Southwest Airlines (LUV)

filingqualifies
Other revenues for 2025 increased by $29 million, or 1.2 percent, compared with 2024. On both a dollar basis and a per unit basis, the increase was primarily due to a $30 million increase in loyalty revenues due to a higher portion of revenues recognized sooner associated with the Company's co-brand agreement with Chase, as a result of the contract amendments with Chase in 2025.
— LUV 10-K FY2025, Item 7 MD&A, Operating Revenues, 2026-02
